Provenance note: Extracted unaltered: the item's own djvu.txt is reproduced whole after this header; nothing was stripped, normalized or corrected. Rights basis: published 1780, out of US copyright by date. OCR quality: legible, with the long s read as f throughout; verse and two-column lists run together in places. Princeton Theological Seminary library plates stand at OCR lines 1-26. Word count of the host text: 126,632. Line numbers cited in this header are lines of the host OCR; add 12 for this file's own line numbers.
Content note: Twelve chapters. Dedication to Mrs. Elizabeth Abney at OCR lines 69-142; preface at 143-227, signed Hoxton-Square, 11 April 1780; contents at 228. Chapter I at 306, II 1176, III 2869, IV 4273, V 5919, VI 7503, VII 9183, VIII 11677, IX 13330, X 14208, XI 15158. Chapter XII, from 15791 to 20910, is 57 numbered letters to or about Watts, by Secker and others, not by Watts. Watts's own letters are quoted inside the chapters (for example to his brother at about 3143). Appendix I, a catalogue of his writings, at 20911; Appendix II at 21337; 'Finis' at 21782; Gibbons's list of his own books follows to the end.

PREFACE. 


THE  following  Memoirs  of  Dr.  Watts 
are  derived  from  the  manufcripts 
given  me  by  himfelf,  and  his  Brother  Mr. 
Enoch  Watts — from  the  various  intelligence 
I  have  obtained  from  others— and  from  my 
intimate  acquaintance  with  him  for  feveral 
years  before  his  death.  In  the  courfe  of 
the  work  I  have  largely  intermixed  my 
obfervations  upon  his  writings,  and  inferted 
feveral  compofures  to  his  honour  by  dif- 
ferent authors  both  in  profe  and  verfe. 

The  numerous  original  letters  which 
conclude  the  volume  are  not  only  very  va- 
luable in  themfelves,  but  abundantly  fhow 
in  what  diftinguifhed  efteem  the  DoSlor 
was  held  by  perfons  of  eminent  abilities, 
and  therefore  the  beft  qualified  judges  of 
the  accomplishments  of  others.

As  an  improvement  of  our  chapter  let  me 
entreat  all  perfons,  who  intend  a  learned  pro- 
feffion,  and  efpecially  that  of  divinity,  to  make 
themfelves  well  acquainted  with  the  Latin  and 
Greek  languages.  Hereby  an  ample  and  folid 
foundation  is  laid  for  the  fuperftru&ure  which 
is  to  be  erected  on  it.  The  works  of  nume- 
rous writers  of  the  firft  character  and  benefit 
are  compofed  in  one  or  other  of  them,  and  a 
thorough  acquaintance  with  thefe  authors, 
which  can  only  be  attained  by  a  maftery  or  the 
tongues  in  which  they  wrote,  will  wonderfully 
enlarge  our  flores  of  knowledge,  and  perhaps 
contribute  not  a  little  to  infufe  dignity  and  ele- 
gance into  our  compofitions.  .  Had  not  Dr. 
Watts  been  the  good  claflical  fcholar  he  was 
he  might  never  have  fhone  with  fuch  a  pre* 
eminent  luitre  as  a  poet  and  fine  writer.  By 
his  early  proficiency  in  the  Latin  and  Greek  he 
was  not  only  prepared  for  academical  exercifes

quet on  a  Latin  or  Greek  writer,  the  rinds  in

ly opened  their  internal  treafures  to  him.

jpecies  is  to  be  comprehended.  The  notion  of 
truth  lies  in  congruity  or  agreement  between 
one  thing  and  another.  Thus  Phy fecal  truth  is 
the  conformity  of  a  body  with  the  principles 
whence  it  originated ;  Logical  truth  is  the  agree- 
ment of  the  idea  in  the  mind  with  the  object  $ 
and  Ethical  truth  is  the  harmony  of  our  words 
with  our  actions,  and  of  our  actions  with  our 
words ;  analogous  to  which  is  the  truth  of  God, 
for  it  belongs  to  that  divifion  of  the  Divine  at- 
tributes which  is  flyled  moral,  becaufe  virtues 
analogous  to  thefe  attributes  are  by  the  moral

count I  maybe  bold  to  fay  that  the  conformity

truthj  I  fay,  with  things,  paft,  prefent,  and  to 
come,  whether  they  are  things  actually  per- 
formed, whether  they  are  particular  decrees,

tilings,  or  whether  they  are  future  things  to  be 
effected,  or  only  permitted.  That  God  is  true

form, or  which  (hall  not  be  performed  by  him.

It  may  not  be  improper  to  obferve  that  theie 
additions  which  the  Doftor  made  to  the  poems 
of  others  are  not  to  be  confidered,  as  is  evident 
from  what  he  fays  in  his  preface  to  the  free-

pofitions,  felt  his  mufe  kindled  by  them,  in-

the  flame  where  it  appeared  too  languid,  and 
enlarging  the  defcriptions  where  he  found  them 
not  in  full  proportion  to  their  objects.  In  a 
word,  becaufe  they  were  fine  productions,  there- 
fore he  beftowed  his  pains,  and  hereby  virtually 
palTed  his  higheft  encomiums  upon  them.
